I came in because I just… I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Like I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting, and then I’ll hear a pump vibrating weirdly, or someone makes a side comment, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. I’ll go chase it for hours.

And meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the call, the meeting, the data review—just kind of falls apart. It’s embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping it together.

I’m not really sure. I mean, school was… there were things I could lock onto, like if a teacher said something odd about light or rocks or whatever, I’d go home and read about that instead of doing the worksheet. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not focusing.

So… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.

Not exactly like wandering, no. It feels more like something hooks me.

Like I’ll be in a grant call, and I know I’m supposed to be listening, but then there’s a vibration from one of the pumps, and my brain just goes, wait, that’s wrong, what is that? Or a student makes some little comment that reminds me of an old spectrum with a weird peak, and suddenly I’m pulling files or checking something in the lab instead of doing the thing I was actually supposed to do.

And then hours go by. It’s not casual daydreaming. It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.

But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not focusing.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because kids are kind of scattered anyway, right? And my teachers mostly just said I was bright but “off in my own world,” which—honestly, that phrase always annoyed me. I’d be in the world, just maybe not the part they wanted me in.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have social situations usually felt hard for you to read?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “hard to read” is kind of broad, isn’t it? Sometimes I know exactly what someone means, like in a meeting when someone says “interesting” and they actually mean “please stop talking,” which is—honestly, scientists are terrible about that.\n\nBut other times I miss things, maybe. Or I think I miss them after the fact. It depends on the room, and the stakes, and whether people are being direct. I wouldn’t say I’ve always been confused socially, exactly… I just don’t know how to give you a clean answer there.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled away from what you’re supposed to be doing, does it usually feel like your attention simply wanders on its own?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like wandering, no. It feels more like something hooks me.\n\nLike I’ll be in a grant call, and I know I’m supposed to be listening, but then there’s a vibration from one of the pumps, and my brain just goes, wait, that’s wrong, what is that? Or a student makes some little comment that reminds me of an old spectrum with a weird peak, and suddenly I’m pulling files or checking something in the lab instead of doing the thing I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd then hours go by. It’s not casual daydreaming. Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like a thought I hate and have to obey. It’s more that something grabs the whole front of my mind and suddenly it feels like the only real thing.

That’s actually why I came in, because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s something people need from me, but then I hear a pump vibrating wrong, or a student says some little side thing, or I remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and I’m gone. I’m chasing that for hours.

And meanwhile the thing I was supposed to do is just… falling apart. Like everyone’s waiting, or the meeting moves on, or I miss what I was responsible for. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.

Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.

It’s embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have a hard time staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, school was… there were things I could lock onto, like if a teacher said something odd about light or rocks or whatever, I’d go home and read about that instead of doing the worksheet. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not focusing.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because kids are kind of scattered anyway, right? And my teachers mostly just said I was bright but “off in my own world,” which—honestly, that phrase always annoyed me. I’d be in the world, just maybe not the part they wanted me in.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have social situations usually felt hard for you to read?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “hard to read” is kind of broad, isn’t it? Sometimes I know exactly what someone means, like in a meeting when someone says “interesting” and they actually mean “please stop talking,” which is—honestly, scientists are terrible about that.\n\nBut other times I miss things, maybe. Or I think I miss them after the fact. It depends on the room, and the stakes, and whether people are being direct. I wouldn’t say I’ve always been confused socially, exactly… I just don’t know how to give you a clean answer there.\n\nDoctor: When you get pulled away from what you’re supposed to be doing, does it usually feel like your attention simply wanders on its own?\n\nPatient: Not exactly like wandering, no. It feels more like something hooks me.\n\nLike I’ll be in a grant call, and I know I’m supposed to be listening, but then there’s a vibration from one of the pumps, and my brain just goes, wait, that’s wrong, what is that? Or a student makes some little comment that reminds me of an old spectrum with a weird peak, and suddenly I’m pulling files or checking something in the lab instead of doing the thing I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd then hours go by. It’s not casual daydreaming. It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like an unwanted thought you have to follow even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like a thought I hate and have to obey. It’s more that something grabs the whole front of my mind and suddenly it feels like the only real thing.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s something people need from me, but then I hear a pump vibrating wrong, or a student says some little side thing, or I remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and I’m gone. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing I was supposed to do is just… falling apart. Like everyone’s waiting, or the meeting moves on, or I miss what I was responsible for. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think so. For months now I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, if that makes sense. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I mean, I used to need real sleep, or I thought I did, but lately it’s been… two, maybe three hours most nights.\n\nSometimes I don’t even properly go to bed. I’ll wake up on the couch with the laptop still open, and it’s not like waking up wrecked. I wake up sharp, like, okay, good, now I can get back to it. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think so. For months now I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, if that makes sense. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I mean, I used to need real sleep, or I thought I did, but lately it’s been… two, maybe three hours most nights.\n\nSometimes I don’t even properly go to bed. I’ll wake up on the couch with the laptop still open, and it’s not like waking up wrecked. I wake up sharp, like, okay, good, now I can get back to it. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Yes. That part, yes.

I’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.

And then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.

It’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.

It feels more like something hooks me.\n\nLike I’ll be in a grant call, and I know I’m supposed to be listening, but then there’s a vibration from one of the pumps, and my brain just goes, wait, that’s wrong, what is that? Or a student makes some little comment that reminds me of an old spectrum with a weird peak, and suddenly I’m pulling files or checking something in the lab instead of doing the thing I was actually supposed to do.\n\nAnd then hours go by. It’s not casual daydreaming. It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like an unwanted thought you have to follow even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like a thought I hate and have to obey. It’s more that something grabs the whole front of my mind and suddenly it feels like the only real thing.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s something people need from me, but then I hear a pump vibrating wrong, or a student says some little side thing, or I remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and I’m gone. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing I was supposed to do is just… falling apart. Like everyone’s waiting, or the meeting moves on, or I miss what I was responsible for. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think so. For months now I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, if that makes sense. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I mean, I used to need real sleep, or I thought I did, but lately it’s been… two, maybe three hours most nights.\n\nSometimes I don’t even properly go to bed. I’ll wake up on the couch with the laptop still open, and it’s not like waking up wrecked. I wake up sharp, like, okay, good, now I can get back to it. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring these energized periods, have you had thoughts or beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Inflated_Self_Esteem\nmanifestation: \"I genuinely believed I could rescue the entire multi-site project by myself, redesign the spectroscopy approach, mentor every student better than their advisors, and still beat the grant deadline if people would just stop getting in my way.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.

I really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.

And I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. I remember thinking, if they would just get out of my way for forty-eight hours, I could make it all work.

Which is ridiculous, because even ordering replacement tubing takes three approvals and someone always chooses the wrong diameter—sorry, that’s not the point. The point is yes, people did say I was overestimating what I could do. But I didn’t believe them at the time.

advisors, and still beat the grant deadline if people would just stop getting in my way.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: Most nights I sleep two or three hours, sometimes on the couch with my laptop open, and I wake up sharp and ready to work instead of tired, like sleep is an inefficient interruption.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle because missed priorities, confused scheduling, and strained supervision started putting the project and lab safety expectations at risk.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about seven months, since I took over the multi-site spectroscopy project.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 42 / physical_scientist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.\n\nI really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. I remember thinking, if they would just get out of my way for forty-eight hours, I could make it all work.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, because even ordering replacement tubing takes three approvals and someone always chooses the wrong diameter—sorry, that’s not the point. I don’t think so. I mean, not like seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, or hearing a voice talking to me.

Sometimes when I’m overtired I’ll misread something in the corner of my eye—like a shadow from the equipment rack, or the little status lights on instruments after hours, which honestly all start to look like they’re signaling something if you stare at them long enough. But that’s not really… I don’t think that’s what you mean.

It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like an unwanted thought you have to follow even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. The point is yes, people did say I was overestimating what I could do. I… I don’t know. I don’t think I was admitted to a psychiatric hospital, at least not in the way you mean, like staying on a ward.

There were times people were very concerned and there were conversations about getting me evaluated, but “admitted” feels like a specific box on a form, and I don’t want to tick the wrong box. Hospitals have all these categories—observation, intake, voluntary, involuntary—and nobody explains them clearly unless you already know the system, which is absurd.

So… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of being actually admitted to a psychiatric hospital during one of those periods.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I just… I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. Like I’ll be on a grant call, or in a safety meeting, and then I’ll hear a pump vibrating weirdly, or someone makes a side comment, or I remember some odd peak in an old spectrum, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. I’ll go chase it for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing everyone actually needed from me—the call, the meeting, the data review—just kind of falls apart. It’s embarrassing, because I’m supposed to be the one keeping it together.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you also have a hard time staying focused?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, school was… there were things I could lock onto, like if a teacher said something odd about light or rocks or whatever, I’d go home and read about that instead of doing the worksheet. But I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not focusing.\n\nIt’s hard to compare, because kids are kind of scattered anyway, right? And my teachers mostly just said I was bright but “off in my own world,” which—honestly, that phrase always annoyed me. I’d be in the world, just maybe not the part they wanted me in.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t give you a clean yes or no.\n\nDoctor: Have social situations usually felt hard for you to read?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, “hard to read” is kind of broad, isn’t it? It’s not casual daydreaming. It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like an unwanted thought you have to follow even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like a thought I hate and have to obey. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s something people need from me, but then I hear a pump vibrating wrong, or a student says some little side thing, or I remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and I’m gone. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing I was supposed to do is just… falling apart. Like everyone’s waiting, or the meeting moves on, or I miss what I was responsible for. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think so. For months now I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, if that makes sense. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I mean, I used to need real sleep, or I thought I did, but lately it’s been… two, maybe three hours most nights.\n\nSometimes I don’t even properly go to bed. I’ll wake up on the couch with the laptop still open, and it’s not like waking up wrecked. I wake up sharp, like, okay, good, now I can get back to it. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you had thoughts or beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.\n\nI really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. The point is yes, people did say I was overestimating what I could do. But I didn’t believe them at the time.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said were not actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Pumps click, freezers hum, someone leaves a centrifuge lid badly seated and then everyone pretends they don’t hear it until it becomes my problem. Sorry—no. As far as I know, I haven’t heard or seen things that other people said weren’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of one of these energized periods?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it in those terms—like, “because of an energized period.” Hospitals to me are all fluorescent lights and bad signage and someone telling you to take a number even though there are only two chairs in the room. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I don’t remember anyone taking me to an ER specifically because of my behavior during one of those periods.

There were times people wanted me to “go get checked,” but that phrase can mean anything—urgent care, occupational health, the ER, calling someone’s cousin who’s a nurse. And emergency rooms are chaotic; I avoid them if I can. The waiting alone feels like being trapped in an airport that forgot it was an airport.

So… I don’t have a clear yes. I can’t say I remember being taken to an emergency room for that.

It’s not casual daydreaming. It’s like the other thing becomes urgent, even if the meeting or review is what actually matters.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, does it feel like an unwanted thought you have to follow even though you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: Not exactly unwanted like… intrusive, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like a thought I hate and have to obey. It’s more that something grabs the whole front of my mind and suddenly it feels like the only real thing.\n\nThat’s actually why I came in, because I can’t keep my mind on the actual experiment anymore. I’ll be on a grant call or in a safety meeting, and I know there’s something people need from me, but then I hear a pump vibrating wrong, or a student says some little side thing, or I remember an odd peak in an old spectrum, and I’m gone. I’m chasing that for hours.\n\nAnd meanwhile the thing I was supposed to do is just… falling apart. Like everyone’s waiting, or the meeting moves on, or I miss what I was responsible for. It doesn’t feel optional once it catches me, but it doesn’t feel like an unwanted thought exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period when you felt unusually energized or high, compared with your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, I think so. For months now I’ve felt like the lab is finally moving at my speed, if that makes sense. Like everything is charged, almost electrified, and I can see how it all should fit together.\n\nBut then if someone questions the plan, or asks me to slow down—like a student saying we should “revisit the timeline,” which is such a committee phrase—I just snap. Too hard, honestly. I’ll hear myself being sharp and then they stop coming by my office unless they absolutely have to. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you had thoughts or beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.\n\nI really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. So yes, unusually energized, but not always in a pleasant way.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you need less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I mean, I used to need real sleep, or I thought I did, but lately it’s been… two, maybe three hours most nights.\n\nSometimes I don’t even properly go to bed. I’ll wake up on the couch with the laptop still open, and it’s not like waking up wrecked. I wake up sharp, like, okay, good, now I can get back to it. Honestly sleep has started to feel like this inefficient interruption, which I know sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nBut yes, less sleep. A lot less.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you feel unusually confident in your abilities or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yes. At the time it didn’t feel like “confidence,” it felt like I was just… accurately seeing what needed to be done and that I was the only one willing to do it.\n\nI really believed I could rescue the whole multi-site project by myself. Not just my piece of it—the entire thing. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the timeline, mentor all the students better than their actual advisors were doing, and still beat the grant deadline.\n\nAnd if people would just stop slowing everything down with meetings and cautious little emails, I thought I could pull it off. I can hear how that sounds now, but in the moment it felt completely obvious.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself talking much more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I talk a lot at work anyway—lectures, meetings, explaining instruments to people who pretend they read the manual but clearly didn’t—so it’s hard for me to know what counts as “much more.”\n\nMy students might say yes, probably, but students also think any explanation longer than thirty seconds is a monologue. Sorry, that’s not really the point. I guess I haven’t noticed it clearly the way I noticed the energy. I don’t know. What do you mean exactly—like talking fast, or just talking more often?\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do other people tell you that you’re talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that anyone’s said it in those exact words. People will sort of smile or cut in, or my lab manager gives me this look like, “Are you finished?”—but she also gives that look when the vacuum pump is making that horrible chirping noise, so it’s not necessarily about me.\n\nI mean, my sister has told me to slow down before, but that might’ve been more because I was explaining something technical over dinner, and honestly nobody wants a lecture about calibration drift while they’re eating. So… I guess I’m not sure. No one has clearly said, “You’re talking more than usual,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this unusually energized, low-sleep state feel clearly different from your normal self?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, clearly. It’s not just “I had a productive week” or drank too much coffee. It feels like the whole lab is finally moving at my speed, like everything is electrified and I can see the path through all the mess.\n\nAnd then—sorry, this is where I sound awful—if someone questions the plan or asks me to slow down, I snap. Not just get annoyed, but sharp enough that students have started avoiding my office, I think. Which is ridiculous, because my office is where the spectra are, and also where the decent stapler is, but… yes. It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you had thoughts or beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.\n\nI really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. I remember thinking, if they would just get out of my way for forty-eight hours, I could make it all work.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, because even ordering replacement tubing takes three approvals and someone always chooses the wrong diameter—sorry, that’s not the point. The point is yes, people did say I was overestimating what I could do. But I didn’t believe them at the time.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said were not actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. But that’s not really… I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nAnd labs make weird noises anyway. Pumps click, freezers hum, someone leaves a centrifuge lid badly seated and then everyone pretends they don’t hear it until it becomes my problem. Sorry—no. As far as I know, I haven’t heard or seen things that other people said weren’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of one of these energized periods?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it in those terms—like, “because of an energized period.” Hospitals to me are all fluorescent lights and bad signage and someone telling you to take a number even though there are only two chairs in the room. Sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t exactly that, and I don’t want to say no if someone else would describe it differently. I’d probably need you to be more specific about what you mean by hospitalized.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, were you admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I was admitted to a psychiatric hospital, at least not in the way you mean, like staying on a ward.\n\nThere were times people were very concerned and there were conversations about getting me evaluated, but “admitted” feels like a specific box on a form, and I don’t want to tick the wrong box. Hospitals have all these categories—observation, intake, voluntary, involuntary—and nobody explains them clearly unless you already know the system, which is absurd.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of being actually admitted to a psychiatric hospital during one of those periods.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, did anyone take you to an emergency room because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t remember anyone taking me to an ER specifically because of my behavior during one of those periods.\n\nThere were times people wanted me to “go get checked,” but that phrase can mean anything—urgent care, occupational health, the ER, calling someone’s cousin who’s a nurse. I can’t say I remember being taken to an emergency room for that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… work especially. Relationships too, I guess, but work is easier to point to without it sounding like I’m being dramatic.

My department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. Things like priorities getting missed, scheduling getting confused—like people thinking something was happening Tuesday when I’d apparently told someone else Thursday, and then suddenly everyone is staring at the same calendar like it personally betrayed us.

It feels very different from my normal self.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you done things that were unusually risky or impulsive for you?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that. “Risky” is a tricky word, because in my work people call something risky if you move a piece of equipment six inches without having a meeting about it first.\n\nImpulsive, maybe in the sense that I’ll decide something needs doing immediately and I don’t want to wait for everyone to catch up—but I don’t know if that’s what you mean. Like, unusually reckless? I can’t think of a clear example off the top of my head. My brain keeps going to lab safety, which is probably not what you’re asking, and also now I’m thinking about the time someone labeled ethanol with masking tape, which should be illegal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed myself doing anything I’d clearly call risky, but maybe you’d have to tell me what kinds of things you mean.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, do you find yourself starting far more projects or taking on much more work than you normally would?\n\nPatient: Yes. That part, yes.\n\nI’ll get to the lab before anyone else is there—like before the building has really woken up—and suddenly there are eight things that all seem urgent and obvious. I’ll start rewriting protocols, moving around the instrument schedule, emailing collaborators in, I don’t know, three different countries because time zones stop seeming like a normal barrier.\n\nAnd then I’ll open new analyses while the other ones are still running, because sitting still feels… wasteful. Like this is the window where I can fix everything if I just keep moving fast enough.\n\nIt’s not always “projects” in the neat grant-proposal sense, though. Sometimes it’s cleaning up a data pipeline, reorganizing samples, making a new tracking spreadsheet—which sounds harmless, except then nobody can find the old one because I decided the naming convention was morally wrong at 5 a.m. So yes, I take on way more than I normally would.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you had thoughts or beliefs that other people said were unrealistic or not true?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. At the time it didn’t feel unrealistic, which I guess is the point.\n\nI really believed I could pull the whole multi-site project back from the edge by myself. Not just help—rescue it. Redesign the spectroscopy approach, fix the analysis, get every site coordinated, mentor the students better than their own advisors were doing, and still somehow beat the grant deadline if everyone would just stop slowing me down.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds now. It sounds… grandiose, maybe. But when I was in it, it felt obvious. Like I was the only one seeing the clean solution while everyone else was tangled up in committees and cautious emails. I remember thinking, if they would just get out of my way for forty-eight hours, I could make it all work.\n\nWhich is ridiculous, because even ordering replacement tubing takes three approvals and someone always chooses the wrong diameter—sorry, that’s not the point. The point is yes, people did say I was overestimating what I could do. But I didn’t believe them at the time.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, have you ever heard or seen things that other people said were not actually there?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, not like seeing a person in the room who isn’t there, or hearing a voice talking to me.\n\nSometimes when I’m overtired I’ll misread something in the corner of my eye—like a shadow from the equipment rack, or the little status lights on instruments after hours, which honestly all start to look like they’re signaling something if you stare at them long enough. But that’s not really… I don’t think that’s what you mean.\n\nAnd labs make weird noises anyway. Pumps click, freezers hum, someone leaves a centrifuge lid badly seated and then everyone pretends they don’t hear it until it becomes my problem. Sorry—no. As far as I know, I haven’t heard or seen things that other people said weren’t actually there.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hospitalized because of one of these energized periods?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly.\n\nI haven’t really thought of it in those terms—like, “because of an energized period.” Hospitals to me are all fluorescent lights and bad signage and someone telling you to take a number even though there are only two chairs in the room. Sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t know. I don’t want to say yes if it wasn’t exactly that, and I don’t want to say no if someone else would describe it differently. I’d probably need you to be more specific about what you mean by hospitalized.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, were you admitted to a psychiatric hospital?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I don’t think I was admitted to a psychiatric hospital, at least not in the way you mean, like staying on a ward.\n\nThere were times people were very concerned and there were conversations about getting me evaluated, but “admitted” feels like a specific box on a form, and I don’t want to tick the wrong box. Hospitals have all these categories—observation, intake, voluntary, involuntary—and nobody explains them clearly unless you already know the system, which is absurd.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I don’t have a clear memory of being actually admitted to a psychiatric hospital during one of those periods.\n\nDoctor: During one of these energized periods, did anyone take you to an emergency room because of your behavior?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t remember anyone taking me to an ER specifically because of my behavior during one of those periods.\n\nThere were times people wanted me to “go get checked,” but that phrase can mean anything—urgent care, occupational health, the ER, calling someone’s cousin who’s a nurse. And emergency rooms are chaotic; I avoid them if I can. The waiting alone feels like being trapped in an airport that forgot it was an airport.\n\nSo… I don’t have a clear yes. I can’t say I remember being taken to an emergency room for that.\n\nDoctor: During these energized periods, did your behavior cause serious problems at work or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… work especially. Relationships too, I guess, but work is easier to point to without it sounding like I’m being dramatic.\n\nMy department has had to step in on decisions I used to handle myself. Not just annoying people, but straining it enough that the project was at risk, and because it’s a lab, “risk” isn’t just paperwork. There are safety expectations.
